### Cross-Modal Watermarking for Authentic Audio Recovery and Tamper Localization in Synthesized Audiovisual Forgeries
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12723v1)
- **Authors**: Minyoung Kim, Sehwan Park, Sungmin Cha, Paul Hongsuck Seo
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in voice cloning and lip synchronization models have enabled Synthesized Audiovisual Forgeries (SAVFs), where both audio and visuals are manipulated to mimic a target speaker. This significantly increases the risk of misinformation by making fake content seem real. To address this issue, existing methods detect or localize manipulations but cannot recover the authentic audio that conveys the semantic content of the message. This limitation reduces their effectiveness in combating audiovisual misinformation. In this work, we introduce the task of Authentic Audio Recovery (AAR) and Tamper Localization in Audio (TLA) from SAVFs and propose a cross-modal watermarking framework to embed authentic audio into visuals before manipulation. This enables AAR, TLA, and a robust defense against misinformation. Extensive experiments demonstrate the strong performance of our method in AAR and TLA against various manipulations, including voice cloning and lip synchronization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel task: Authentic Audio Recovery (AAR) and Tamper Localization in Audio (TLA) from Synthesized Audiovisual Forgeries (SAVFs). The authors propose a cross-modal watermarking framework where authentic audio is embedded into visual frames before potential manipulation. This allows for the recovery of the original audio signal, even if the audio stream is altered or replaced. The method also aids in localizing tampered regions by comparing the recovered audio with the tampered audio. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of the approach through extensive experiments against various manipulations, including voice cloning and lip synchronization. The framework also exhibits robustness even when trained on datasets without human faces or voices, addressing privacy concerns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The introduction of the AAR task itself is relatively novel. While audio tamper detection and localization exist, actively recovering the *authentic* audio adds a significant dimension. Using cross-modal watermarking for this specific purpose (AAR and TLA simultaneously) within the SAVF context is also a valuable contribution. The privacy-preserving aspect of training without human faces or voices is a noteworthy practical consideration.

*   **Significance:** The rise of deepfakes and SAVFs poses a significant threat to information integrity. A method that not only detects tampering but also recovers the authentic message has high practical value. The tamper localization aspect further enhances the utility by identifying specific manipulated regions. The focus on recovering semantic content instead of just identifying anomalies is a key differentiator from prior work. The results presented seem to surpass recent works.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the AAR and TLA tasks.
    *   **Cross-modal Approach:** Leveraging visual information to protect and recover audio demonstrates a clever insight.
    *   **Robustness:** The method shows strong performance against various SAVF techniques, including voice cloning and lip synchronization, indicated by the comprehensive metrics.
    *   **Privacy Considerations:** The ability to train on non-human datasets is a significant advantage for real-world deployment.
    *   **Well-defined architecture**: The watermarking mechanism is described and explained.
    *   **Good ablations**: The authors demonstrate the robustness of their proposed approach against alternative baselines, as well as the importance of the masking strategies adopted.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on INNs:** The performance of the method is heavily reliant on the INN architecture, which can be computationally expensive and complex to train. The dependence on these specific network choices could limit broader adoption.
    *   **Limited Tampering Types:** The experiments primarily focus on voice cloning and lip synchronization. Performance against other types of audio manipulations (e.g., speech synthesis, splicing) could be further explored.
    *   **SNR and PESQ are not perfect metrics**: Subjective assessment with human evaluators would further strengthen the experimental results, although the quantitative results are promising.
    *   **Limited visual information usage**: Since audio is the target task, the information extracted from the visual is not clearly utilized.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of deepfake detection and countermeasures. The concept of authentic audio recovery could inspire new research directions focused on restoring manipulated content rather than simply detecting it. The cross-modal watermarking technique could be adapted for other multimodal forgery detection tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of deepfake detection. The introduction of the AAR task, the cross-modal watermarking approach, the robustness against various SAVF techniques, and the consideration of privacy issues all contribute to its value. Although the reliance on INNs and the limited evaluation against different manipulation types are weaknesses, the overall strengths outweigh these drawbacks. It provides a proactive, practical solution to a growing problem. The paper contributes to a more resilient digital ecosystem by enabling the recovery of truthful information, rather than merely detecting falsehoods.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 8/10

### osmAG-LLM: Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Object Navigation via Semantic Maps and Large Language Models Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12753v1)
- **Authors**: Fujing Xie, Sören Schwertfeger, Hermann Blum
- **Abstract**: Recent open-vocabulary robot mapping methods enrich dense geometric maps with pre-trained visual-language features, achieving a high level of detail and guiding robots to find objects specified by open-vocabulary language queries. While the issue of scalability for such approaches has received some attention, another fundamental problem is that high-detail object mapping quickly becomes outdated, as objects get moved around a lot. In this work, we develop a mapping and navigation system for object-goal navigation that, from the ground up, considers the possibilities that a queried object can have moved, or may not be mapped at all. Instead of striving for high-fidelity mapping detail, we consider that the main purpose of a map is to provide environment grounding and context, which we combine with the semantic priors of LLMs to reason about object locations and deploy an active, online approach to navigate to the objects. Through simulated and real-world experiments we find that our approach tends to have higher retrieval success at shorter path lengths for static objects and by far outperforms prior approaches in cases of dynamic or unmapped object queries. We provide our code and dataset at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/osmAG-LLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "osmAG-LLM: Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Object Navigation via Semantic Maps and Large Language Models Reasoning" presents a novel approach to object-goal navigation for robots in indoor environments.  It combines the hierarchical semantic and topometric map representation *osmAG* (an extension of OpenStreetMap tailored for indoor spaces) with the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The system uses an LLM to interpret natural language queries, predict probable object locations within the map, and guide the robot's navigation.  Crucially, the system addresses the common problem of object movement and unmapped objects by incorporating online object detection and VLM-based verification. The approach is evaluated in both simulated (HM3D-SEM) and real-world environments, demonstrating improved retrieval success rates, shorter path lengths, and resilience to dynamic environments compared to a hierarchical open-vocabulary scene graph approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in the specific combination of existing technologies and the comprehensive approach to address object navigation.  While individual components like osmAG, LLMs for reasoning, and open-vocabulary object detection are not entirely new, their integration, particularly the use of a text-based semantic map directly parsable by LLMs and the online verification step, is a significant contribution. The authors acknowledge that existing approaches tackle subproblems, but the comprehensive system built addresses limitations of individual components. The use of vision language models to provide semantic object and room descriptions with uncertainty to the mapping process is also novel.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its ability to make object-goal navigation more robust and adaptable to real-world, dynamic environments. The focus on a scalable, text-based map representation that can be readily integrated with LLMs has practical implications for large-scale deployments. The improved retrieval success rate, especially for unmapped and relocated objects, directly addresses a crucial challenge in robotics: dealing with incomplete or outdated information. Furthermore, this work reduces reliance on high-fidelity 3D point cloud representation for map creation and operation. By utilizing permanent structures for localization and adapting online, their system is more robust to environmental dynamics.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive System:**  The paper presents a complete and functional system, integrating mapping, reasoning, and navigation.
    *   **Addressing a Key Problem:** The system tackles the real-world issue of object movement and uncertainty in maps, a significant limitation of many current navigation systems.
    *   **Improved Performance:** Both simulated and real-world experiments demonstrate clear performance improvements over a reasonable baseline.
    *   **Scalability Potential:** The text-based map representation and use of LLMs suggest potential for scalability to larger environments.
    *   **Ablation Studies and Failure analysis**: provides a deeper understanding of the contribution of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The system's performance is inevitably tied to the capabilities of the LLMs used. The LLM prompt and internal knowledge base could impact retrieval outcomes.
    *   **Limited Real-World Experiments:** While real-world experiments are conducted, they are limited to a single campus building and a relatively small set of queries. Expanding these experiments would further validate the system's robustness.
    *   **Online Object Detection Failures:**  The failure analysis indicates limitations in the object detection and VLM-based verification components, suggesting areas for further improvement. The system relies on the accuracy of the VLMs and open-vocabulary object detectors and could be sensitive to failures of these components.
    *   **Incremental nature**: The work is based on several components already introduced, and the novelty is more about the way they are combined.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in object-goal navigation by highlighting the importance of incorporating LLM reasoning and online perception to address the challenges of dynamic environments. The osmAG-LLM approach could serve as a template for developing more robust and scalable navigation systems. It could influence future research looking at reducing reliance on detailed 3D scans for map creation, instead using a semantic map and online adaptation.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a well-executed and significant contribution to the field of object-goal navigation. The comprehensive system, demonstrated improvements in performance, and focus on addressing real-world challenges justify a high score. The integration of existing technologies, though not revolutionary individually, is done thoughtfully and results in a notable advance. A 7 is assigned due to the clear improvements demonstrated and the impact on the field, while acknowledging that future iterations can be done to further strengthen the experimental validation and robustness of individual components of the system. Additionally, the novelty is high, but it is more about the ensemble of components than groundbreaking novel sub-components.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Logit Arithmetic Elicits Long Reasoning Capabilities Without Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12759v1)
- **Authors**: Yunxiang Zhang, Muhammad Khalifa, Lechen Zhang, Xin Liu, Ayoung Lee, Xinliang Frederick Zhang, Farima Fatahi Bayat, Lu Wang
- **Abstract**: Large reasoning models (LRMs) can do complex reasoning via long chain-of-thought (CoT) involving cognitive strategies such as backtracking and self-correction. Recent studies suggest that some models inherently possess these long reasoning abilities, which may be unlocked via extra training. Our work first investigates whether we can elicit such behavior without any training. To this end, we propose a decoding-time approach, ThinkLogit, which utilizes logits arithmetic (Liu et al., 2024) to tune a target large LM for long reasoning using a substantially smaller model as guider. We then show that we can further boost performance by training the guider model with preference optimization over correct/incorrect reasoning pairs sampled from both the target and guider model -- a setup we refer to as ThinkLogit-DPO. Our experiments demonstrate that ThinkLogit and ThinkLogit-DPO achieve a relative improvement in pass@1 by 26% and 29%, respectively, over four mathematical datasets using the Qwen2.5-32B when guided by R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B -- a model 21x smaller. Lastly, we show that ThinkLogit can transfer long reasoning skills acquired through reinforcement learning, improving pass@1 by 13% relative compared to the Qwen2.5-32B base model. Our work presents a computationally-efficient method to elicit long reasoning in large models with minimal or no additional training.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "ThinkLogit" and "ThinkLogit-DPO," two computationally efficient decoding-time methods to elicit long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning capabilities from large language models (LLMs) without extensive training. ThinkLogit uses logit arithmetic to combine the output distributions of a smaller, reasoning-capable "guider" model with a larger, less-reasoning capable target model. ThinkLogit-DPO refines this approach by training the guider model using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) on preference pairs sampled from both models to better align them.  Experiments show that these methods significantly improve the performance of LLMs on mathematical reasoning tasks, achieving performance gains comparable to or near full-parameter fine-tuning while using significantly less training data and compute.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core idea of using logit arithmetic to guide a larger model with a smaller one is inspired by existing work on logits arithmetic.  However, the specific application to *long* CoT reasoning and the focus on *decoding-time* elicitation (without finetuning the large model) is relatively novel. The ThinkLogit-DPO aspect, which strategically aligns the guider using preference data tailored to large model-small model cooperation, adds a further layer of novelty. The authors use a limited number of preference data pairs to boost performance, highlighting the importance of effective training data.

*   **Significance:** The work has practical significance. Training LLMs for complex reasoning is computationally expensive. A method that can unlock existing reasoning capabilities in LLMs at inference time, with minimal training or parameter updates, is a valuable contribution. The results indicate a promising direction for improving the reasoning abilities of pre-trained models without incurring substantial training costs. The paper also highlights the importance of using small and effective training data rather than large-scale parameter tuning.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Computational Efficiency:** A key strength is the method's efficiency. It avoids the expensive full model finetuning required by many other approaches to long-CoT reasoning.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents convincing empirical evidence, demonstrating significant performance gains on challenging mathematical reasoning benchmarks, compared against strong baselines.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The approach is clearly explained and well-motivated. The combination of ThinkLogit with DPO is a logical extension.
    *   **Ablation Studies:**  The ablation studies help understand the contributions of different components of the approach. It demonstrates the value of both warm-up and effective tuning data.
    *   **Readability and clarity:** The paper is well-organized and clearly written.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Domain Specificity:** The evaluation is limited to mathematical reasoning.  While this is a challenging and important domain, it's unclear how well the approach would generalize to other reasoning tasks or different types of data (e.g., commonsense reasoning, knowledge-based QA). Future experiments should be conducted in other domains to evaluate the effectiveness of the method.
    *   **Reliance on a Good "Guider" Model:** The method's success depends heavily on the availability of a small, reasoning-capable guider model. It would be valuable to explore methods for creating or adapting guider models more efficiently. The paper also doesn't investigate the effect of different guider model sizes, or the relationship between the guider model's capabilities and the final performance.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Logit Interactions:** Although the paper uses Logit Arithmetic as its core element, it is unclear how the guidance process interacts with the large model's internal representations. A more detailed study of how the guider changes logit distribution and whether the large model is "learning" anything at decoding time is missing.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a practical and efficient method for improving the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. It could influence future research on:

    *   Decoding-time interventions for LLMs.
    *   Logit arithmetic and other techniques for model fusion.
    *   Training data strategies for aligning smaller models with larger ones.
    *   Methods for eliciting latent capabilities from pre-trained models.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM reasoning. The ThinkLogit and ThinkLogit-DPO methods offer a computationally efficient approach for eliciting long-CoT reasoning capabilities without full model fine-tuning, and the results are compelling.

However, there are some limitations in terms of generalizability (domain-specificity) and dependency on a suitable guider model. Also, the method builds on previous works using Logit Arithmetic and DPO, and could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the logit interactions and the specific reasons behind the success of the chosen preference pairs. Future work will need to address these limitations to fully realize the potential of the proposed approach.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Think-Before-Draw: Decomposing Emotion Semantics & Fine-Grained Controllable Expressive Talking Head Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12761v1)
- **Authors**: Hanlei Shi, Leyuan Qu, Yu Liu, Di Gao, Yuhua Zheng, Taihao Li
- **Abstract**: Emotional talking-head generation has emerged as a pivotal research area at the intersection of computer vision and multimodal artificial intelligence, with its core value lying in enhancing human-computer interaction through immersive and empathetic engagement.With the advancement of multimodal large language models, the driving signals for emotional talking-head generation has shifted from audio and video to more flexible text. However, current text-driven methods rely on predefined discrete emotion label texts, oversimplifying the dynamic complexity of real facial muscle movements and thus failing to achieve natural emotional expressiveness.This study proposes the Think-Before-Draw framework to address two key challenges: (1) In-depth semantic parsing of emotions--by innovatively introducing Chain-of-Thought (CoT), abstract emotion labels are transformed into physiologically grounded facial muscle movement descriptions, enabling the mapping from high-level semantics to actionable motion features; and (2) Fine-grained expressiveness optimization--inspired by artists' portrait painting process, a progressive guidance denoising strategy is proposed, employing a "global emotion localization--local muscle control" mechanism to refine micro-expression dynamics in generated videos.Our experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance on widely-used benchmarks, including MEAD and HDTF. Additionally, we collected a set of portrait images to evaluate our model's zero-shot generation capability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Think-Before-Draw: Decomposing Emotion Semantics & Fine-Grained Controllable Expressive Talking Head Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of generating expressive talking-head videos driven by text, focusing particularly on improving the naturalness and controllability of emotional expressions. The core contribution is a "Think-Before-Draw" framework that tackles this problem through two main components: (1) a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) module that breaks down abstract emotion labels into physiologically grounded facial muscle movements, effectively translating high-level semantics into actionable motion features; and (2) a progressive guidance denoising strategy, inspired by portrait painting, that refines micro-expression dynamics during video generation using a global-to-local control mechanism. The framework integrates these components into a diffusion-based generative model and demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on benchmarks like MEAD and HDTF, as well as in zero-shot generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonable degree of novelty, primarily through the integration of existing techniques (Chain-of-Thought prompting and diffusion models) in a novel way for talking-head generation. The idea of using CoT to decompose emotional semantics into muscle movements is a valuable step toward more grounded and controllable emotion generation. Also, the progressive guidance denoising strategy mimics human artistic creation, which is innovative.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve the realism and controllability of virtual avatars, enabling more immersive and empathetic human-computer interactions. By addressing the semantic gap between text and facial actions, the work makes it easier to generate nuanced emotional expressions. State-of-the-art performance on benchmark datasets and qualitative results support these claims. The user study also reinforces the usefulness of the method.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing text-driven talking-head generation methods, specifically their inability to capture the complexity of real emotional expressions.
    *   **Well-Designed Framework:** The "Think-Before-Draw" framework is well-structured and integrates CoT and progressive guidance in a logical and effective manner.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough evaluation with quantitative results on standard datasets, qualitative comparisons, and a user study.
    *   **Physiologically-Grounded Approach:** Attempting to ground the emotion generation process in facial muscle movements is a solid and potentially impactful direction.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies demonstrate the value of each component, reinforcing the effectiveness of CoT and the progressive guidance denoising strategy.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on Pretrained Models:** The framework relies on several pretrained models (large language models, diffusion models), and the performance is inherently limited by the capabilities of these underlying models.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Novel Architectures:** The core of the video generation relies on an existing diffusion model, leaving a relatively limited investigation of potentially more effective architectural solutions.
    *   **Focus of Evaluation:** It can be argued that all results are in a simulated scenario, not real-world.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The use of diffusion models and CoT prompting can be computationally expensive, limiting the practicality of the framework for real-time applications.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to impact the field of virtual avatar creation, human-computer interaction, and entertainment. The ability to generate more realistic and controllable emotional expressions can lead to more engaging and immersive experiences in these domains.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution, but is built from existing frameworks and pretrained models. CoT technique and guidance denoising strategy contributes a little novelty. The approach is well-engineered and thoroughly evaluated, and addresses the key problem of controllable and realistic talking-head generation. However, relying on pretrained components limits the innovation.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Local Representative Token Guided Merging for Text-to-Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12771v1)
- **Authors**: Min-Jeong Lee, Hee-Dong Kim, Seong-Whan Lee
- **Abstract**: Stable diffusion is an outstanding image generation model for text-to-image, but its time-consuming generation process remains a challenge due to the quadratic complexity of attention operations. Recent token merging methods improve efficiency by reducing the number of tokens during attention operations, but often overlook the characteristics of attention-based image generation models, limiting their effectiveness. In this paper, we propose local representative token guided merging (ReToM), a novel token merging strategy applicable to any attention mechanism in image generation. To merge tokens based on various contextual information, ReToM defines local boundaries as windows within attention inputs and adjusts window sizes. Furthermore, we introduce a representative token, which represents the most representative token per window by computing similarity at a specific timestep and selecting the token with the highest average similarity. This approach preserves the most salient local features while minimizing computational overhead. Experimental results show that ReToM achieves a 6.2% improvement in FID and higher CLIP scores compared to the baseline, while maintaining comparable inference time. We empirically demonstrate that ReToM is effective in balancing visual quality and computational efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Local Representative Token Guided Merging" (ReToM), a novel token merging strategy to improve the efficiency of text-to-image generation using stable diffusion models. ReToM addresses the computational bottleneck caused by quadratic complexity of attention operations.  It does this by (1) adaptively adjusting the window size for token merging based on the U-Net architecture in stable diffusion, (2) selecting a representative token within each window to preserve local feature information, and (3) caching similarity computations to reduce redundant calculations. The experimental results demonstrate that ReToM improves image quality (measured by FID and CLIP scores) while maintaining comparable inference time compared to existing token merging methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents incremental novelty. Token merging itself is not new (ToMeSD). However, the adaptive window size and the representative token selection strategies based on local similarity are novel contributions. The similarity computation caching further boosts the efficiency of these steps.

*   **Significance:** The paper contributes to the ongoing effort of improving the efficiency of stable diffusion models, which is a practically significant problem. Reducing the computational cost of generating high-quality images opens the door to more accessible and real-time applications. While the improvement in FID and CLIP scores is modest, the combination with maintained inference speed makes ReToM a valuable addition.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Adaptability:** ReToM is designed to be easily integrated into existing attention-based models without requiring retraining or fine-tuning.
    *   **Performance:** The experimental results consistently show improved image quality and comparable inference time compared to existing methods.
    *   **Soundness:** The design choices are well-motivated by the structure and operation of stable diffusion models. The adaptive window size and representative token selection are both intuitive and effective.
    *   **Completeness:** The paper thoroughly presents the ReToM framework and offers sufficient details for implementation and evaluation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The improvement over existing token merging techniques, while consistent, is not drastic.  It's an optimization strategy rather than a fundamental breakthrough.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** While the experiments are performed on ImageNet, it's not clear how ReToM generalizes to other datasets or different diffusion model architectures.

*   **Impact:** The paper has potential to be influential within the stable diffusion research community and those interested in model optimization. The ease of integration and the demonstrated efficiency gains make it a practical solution. However, due to the incremental nature of the advance and the limited generalizability demonstrated in the paper, its long-term impact may be limited.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes valuable contributions to the field of efficient text-to-image generation. While the technical novelty is incremental rather than radical, the proposed ReToM strategy is well-motivated, thoroughly evaluated, and demonstrates practical improvements in image quality without sacrificing inference speed. The significance lies in its ability to be readily integrated into existing stable diffusion pipelines. Given these strengths, balanced against the aforementioned weaknesses, a score of 7 is assigned.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Comprehensive Survey of Electronic Health Record Modeling: From Deep Learning Approaches to Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12774v1)
- **Authors**: Weijieying Ren, Jingxi Zhu, Zehao Liu, Tianxiang Zhao, Vasant Honavar
- **Abstract**: Artificial intelligence (AI) has demonstrated significant potential in transforming healthcare through the analysis and modeling of electronic health records (EHRs). However, the inherent heterogeneity, temporal irregularity, and domain-specific nature of EHR data present unique challenges that differ fundamentally from those in vision and natural language tasks. This survey offers a comprehensive overview of recent advancements at the intersection of deep learning, large language models (LLMs), and EHR modeling. We introduce a unified taxonomy that spans five key design dimensions: data-centric approaches, neural architecture design, learning-focused strategies, multimodal learning, and LLM-based modeling systems. Within each dimension, we review representative methods addressing data quality enhancement, structural and temporal representation, self-supervised learning, and integration with clinical knowledge. We further highlight emerging trends such as foundation models, LLM-driven clinical agents, and EHR-to-text translation for downstream reasoning. Finally, we discuss open challenges in benchmarking, explainability, clinical alignment, and generalization across diverse clinical settings. This survey aims to provide a structured roadmap for advancing AI-driven EHR modeling and clinical decision support. For a comprehensive list of EHR-related methods, kindly refer to https://survey-on-tabular-data.github.io/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper is a comprehensive survey of deep learning approaches to electronic health record (EHR) modeling, particularly focusing on the integration of deep learning, large language models (LLMs), and EHR data. It introduces a unified taxonomy that spans data-centric approaches, neural architecture design, learning strategies, multimodal learning, and LLM-based modeling systems. The survey reviews methods for data quality enhancement, structural/temporal representation, self-supervised learning, and clinical knowledge integration. Emerging trends like foundation models, LLM-driven clinical agents, and EHR-to-text translation are highlighted. Finally, the paper discusses open challenges in benchmarking, explainability, clinical alignment, and generalization across diverse clinical settings. The goal is to provide a structured roadmap for advancing AI-driven EHR modeling and clinical decision support.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a significant contribution by consolidating a rapidly evolving field. While surveys on individual components of EHR modeling exist (architectures, clinical language models), this survey offers a comprehensive overview encompassing data-centric strategies, architecture design, learning objectives, and the integration of LLM paradigms. The coarse-to-fine taxonomy is a valuable contribution for understanding the landscape. The coverage of emerging trends like clinical agents is timely.
*   **Significance:** EHR modeling is crucial for transforming healthcare, but faces unique challenges not found in vision or NLP. By structuring the current knowledge, the survey provides a crucial resource for researchers and practitioners. Identifying open challenges (benchmarking, explainability, alignment, generalization) is also vital for directing future research. The detailed taxonomy and links to datasets offer practical value for the community.
*   **Strengths:** The paper's strengths lie in its breadth, clear organization, and timely coverage of LLM-driven approaches. The unified taxonomy provides a structured understanding of different modeling design choices. The inclusion of a companion website for updates is also a significant strength.
*   **Weaknesses:** While comprehensive, the paper could benefit from more in-depth comparisons of different approaches within each category. For instance, a quantitative comparison of the performance of different neural architectures on standard benchmark datasets would be valuable. The discussion of ethical considerations (bias, fairness) could be expanded.
*   **Potential Influence:** The survey is likely to become a widely cited reference in the field, guiding researchers and practitioners in developing more effective and trustworthy AI-driven solutions for healthcare. It also helps to identify areas where innovation is needed. The emphasis on key design dimensions ensures readers consider a holistic approach to EHR modeling.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's value lies primarily in its comprehensive scope, clear organization, and timely consolidation of a rapidly developing field. The taxonomy is a valuable contribution. However, it lacks in-depth comparative analysis of methods (quantitative performance comparisons), and the ethical discussion is high-level. It is well-written and will likely have a strong impact on the field by providing a clear roadmap and identifying critical challenges.

### Compact Vision Transformer by Reduction of Kernel Complexity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12780v1)
- **Authors**: Yancheng Wang, Yingzhen Yang
- **Abstract**: Self-attention and transformer architectures have become foundational components in modern deep learning. Recent efforts have integrated transformer blocks into compact neural architectures for computer vision, giving rise to various efficient vision transformers. In this work, we introduce Transformer with Kernel Complexity Reduction, or KCR-Transformer, a compact transformer block equipped with differentiable channel selection, guided by a novel and sharp theoretical generalization bound. KCR-Transformer performs input/output channel selection in the MLP layers of transformer blocks to reduce the computational cost. Furthermore, we provide a rigorous theoretical analysis establishing a tight generalization bound for networks equipped with KCR-Transformer blocks. Leveraging such strong theoretical results, the channel pruning by KCR-Transformer is conducted in a generalization-aware manner, ensuring that the resulting network retains a provably small generalization error. Our KCR-Transformer is compatible with many popular and compact transformer networks, such as ViT and Swin, and it reduces the FLOPs of the vision transformers while maintaining or even improving the prediction accuracy. In the experiments, we replace all the transformer blocks in the vision transformers with KCR-Transformer blocks, leading to KCR-Transformer networks with different backbones. The resulting TCR-Transformers achieve superior performance on various computer vision tasks, achieving even better performance than the original models with even less FLOPs and parameters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KCR-Transformer (Transformer with Kernel Complexity Reduction), a novel compact transformer block designed to reduce the computational cost of vision transformers. It achieves this by performing differentiable channel selection in the MLP layers of transformer blocks, guided by a tight theoretical generalization bound derived by the authors. The method is compatible with popular architectures like ViT and Swin, and reduces FLOPs while maintaining or improving accuracy. The channel pruning is done in a generalization-aware manner, ensuring that the resulting network retains a small generalization error. Experimental results show superior performance on image classification, object detection, and instance segmentation tasks, even exceeding the performance of original models with fewer parameters and FLOPs. The paper provides a theoretical analysis establishing a sharp generalization bound for KCR-Transformer networks and optimizes the channel selection with an approximate truncated nuclear norm (TNN).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to transformer compression by directly targeting kernel complexity and leveraging differentiable channel selection guided by theoretical bounds. The derivation of a tight generalization bound related to the kernel complexity is significant. The idea of reducing kernel complexity using an approximate TNN is also interesting. Compared to existing pruning techniques for transformers that often rely on empirical observations, this offers a more principled approach. The combination of a theoretical justification for channel selection with an efficient computational method using the Nyström method is a relatively novel combination.

*   **Significance:** Reducing the computational cost of vision transformers is a vital goal, particularly for deployment in resource-constrained environments. The KCR-Transformer achieves this without sacrificing accuracy, and even demonstrates improvements in some cases, making it significant. The theoretical guarantee on generalization error could lead to more robust and reliable compression methods. The ability to seamlessly integrate into existing architectures (ViT, Swin) enhances its practical applicability. By demonstrating gains over the original models, the paper shows promise in pushing the performance boundaries of vision transformers and their compression methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical foundation. The derived generalization bound provides a principled method for channel selection.
    *   Efficient implementation using the Nyström method for approximating kernel complexity.
    *   Empirical results demonstrate performance gains compared to baselines, not just computational savings.
    *   Broad applicability to different transformer architectures.
    *   Detailed experimental setup and analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The theoretical analysis relies on specific assumptions about the DNN architecture (e.g., positive definite kernel, separability in the TNN), which may limit its applicability to all types of vision transformers.
    *   The complexity of approximating the TNN using Nyström method still could be a challenge for extremely large-scale datasets. The paper addresses this point, but more in-depth empirical investigation is still warranted.
    *   Although the method is effective, the performance improvement over previous NTK-SAP methods seem marginal when considered the overall complexity.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in vision transformer compression by providing a more theoretically grounded approach. It opens up directions for further exploring the relationship between kernel complexity and generalization in deep learning. The practical gains in efficiency and accuracy, if consistently observed, could lead to wider adoption of KCR-Transformer-like methods. The paper could encourage more research into combining theoretical insights and practical implementation techniques for model compression.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the paper's novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of **7** is justified. The paper's main contribution is the introduction of the KCR-Transformer with a clear theoretical grounding using kernel complexity. It demonstrates improvements over conventional compression methods. The limitations associated with the theoretical assumptions and computational complexity, however, prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the performance improvements are significant, the NTK-SAP baseline performance is already quite strong, and more extensive ablation studies would strengthen the paper further. Overall, the theoretical results, novel algorithm, and strong experimental performance earn it a good score with clear future research directions.

### Learning Robust Negation Text Representations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12782v1)
- **Authors**: Thinh Hung Truong, Karin Verspoor, Trevor Cohn, Timothy Baldwin
- **Abstract**: Despite rapid adoption of autoregressive large language models, smaller text encoders still play an important role in text understanding tasks that require rich contextualized representations. Negation is an important semantic function that is still not properly captured by such methods, affecting many downstream applications relying on text embeddings. We propose a strategy to improve negation robustness of text encoders, by distilling data from large language models using diverse patterns of negation and hedging. We adopt a standard contrastive learning strategy to finetune a strong BERT-based model, and observe large improvement in negation understanding capabilities while maintaining competitive performance on general benchmarks. In addition, we also show that our method can be adapted to LLMs, leading to improved performance on negation benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of text encoders' insensitivity to negation and hedging, which can negatively impact downstream applications. It proposes a data augmentation strategy using large language models (LLMs) to generate synthetic data containing diverse patterns of negation and hedging.  A standard contrastive learning approach is then used to fine-tune text encoders on this synthetic data. The authors demonstrate significant improvements in negation understanding benchmarks while maintaining competitive performance on general benchmarks.  They also show that this method can be adapted to fine-tune LLMs, improving performance on negation-focused benchmarks.  The core idea is to improve the ability of text encoders to distinguish between sentences and their negated or hedged variants.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its combination of several existing ideas. The use of synthetic data generated by LLMs for fine-tuning smaller models has been explored before.  Contrastive learning is a well-established technique. The key innovation is the deliberate and linguistically informed synthesis of negation and hedging data using LLMs, guided by specific linguistic taxonomies. This targeted data augmentation approach is well-motivated and contributes a valuable element to the existing literature. While individual components have been used, their integration for robustly improving text encoder sensitivity to negation/hedging is notable.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem. The lack of sensitivity to negation and hedging is a well-known limitation of text encoders, potentially leading to semantic anomalies in various downstream applications. The proposed method provides a practical and effective solution to improve this aspect of text encoder performance. Furthermore, the results obtained on the negation-focused benchmarks are substantial, indicating a significant improvement in negation understanding.
    *   The adaptation of the method to LLMs is interesting but shows local degradation on benchmarks, suggesting that the general training is necessary in addition to this step.
    *   The data augmentation stage with LLMs is very important. This results in better and more varied samples than using more simple augmentation with rule-based techniques.
    *   The error analysis of LLMs provides interesting insights, that could be further addressed.
    *   The experiments with diverse models and the data attribution analysis strengthen the paper's claims.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-motivated problem and clear research question.
    *   Linguistically sound approach to data synthesis.
    *   Significant improvements on negation benchmarks.
    *   Competitive performance on general benchmarks.
    *   Ablation studies demonstrate the importance of data diversity and the role of hedging.
    *   Adaptation to LLMs.
    *   Code and data publicly available.
    *   Error analysis of LLM reasoning provides useful insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the data synthesis uses linguistic theory, the process still relies on manual inspection and iterative prompt engineering, which lacks automation. Although acceptable, using automatic prompt engineering would lead to even better results.
    *   Adaptation to LLMs shows performance degradation on some tasks, and the reasons could be further investigated.
    *   Limited exploration of different contrastive learning methods, as the paper focuses on demonstrating the applicability of the generated triples with a single method.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of text representation learning by providing a practical and effective strategy for improving negation and hedging understanding. It could encourage further research on linguistically informed data augmentation techniques and inspire new methods for building more robust text encoders.  The public availability of the HedgeTriple dataset will likely facilitate further research in this area. It provides an immediate way to test and benchmark LLMs and smaller text encoders. The paper will influence tasks such as sentiment analysis, question answering, and information retrieval, where negation and hedging play a critical role.

**Justification:**

The paper represents a solid contribution to the field. It addresses a well-known problem with a carefully designed and linguistically grounded approach. The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights. While the work has some limitations, the overall impact is positive, and it has the potential to influence future research in text representation learning.
