### Argus: Leveraging Multiview Images for Improved 3-D Scene Understanding With Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12916v1)
- **Authors**: Yifan Xu, Chao Zhang, Hanqi Jiang, Xiaoyan Wang, Ruifei Ma, Yiwei Li, Zihao Wu, Zeju Li, Xiangde Liu
- **Abstract**: Advancements in foundation models have made it possible to conduct applications in various downstream tasks. Especially, the new era has witnessed a remarkable capability to extend Large Language Models (LLMs) for tackling tasks of 3D scene understanding. Current methods rely heavily on 3D point clouds, but the 3D point cloud reconstruction of an indoor scene often results in information loss. Some textureless planes or repetitive patterns are prone to omission and manifest as voids within the reconstructed 3D point clouds. Besides, objects with complex structures tend to introduce distortion of details caused by misalignments between the captured images and the dense reconstructed point clouds. 2D multi-view images present visual consistency with 3D point clouds and provide more detailed representations of scene components, which can naturally compensate for these deficiencies. Based on these insights, we propose Argus, a novel 3D multimodal framework that leverages multi-view images for enhanced 3D scene understanding with LLMs. In general, Argus can be treated as a 3D Large Multimodal Foundation Model (3D-LMM) since it takes various modalities as input(text instructions, 2D multi-view images, and 3D point clouds) and expands the capability of LLMs to tackle 3D tasks. Argus involves fusing and integrating multi-view images and camera poses into view-as-scene features, which interact with the 3D features to create comprehensive and detailed 3D-aware scene embeddings. Our approach compensates for the information loss while reconstructing 3D point clouds and helps LLMs better understand the 3D world. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method outperforms existing 3D-LMMs in various downstream tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Argus: Leveraging Multiview Images for Improved 3-D Scene Understanding With Large Language Models" proposes a novel 3D multimodal framework (3D-LMM) that enhances 3D scene understanding by incorporating multi-view 2D images in conjunction with 3D point clouds. The core idea is to compensate for the information loss inherent in 3D point cloud reconstruction by fusing multi-view image data, processed with camera poses to create what the authors term "view-as-scene" features. This framework utilizes a specialized module called a 3D-aware Q-Former, which interacts with both the 3D point cloud features and the "view-as-scene" features. The output of this interaction is then fed into a frozen Large Language Model (LLM) to tackle various 3D scene understanding tasks like 3D-QA, scene description, embodied planning, and 3D visual grounding. The authors demonstrate through experiments that their approach outperforms existing 3D-LMMs on various downstream tasks. The key innovations include the fusion of multi-view images and the 3D-aware Q-Former.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using multi-view images to supplement 3D point clouds for scene understanding is not entirely new, but the paper's specific implementation using the "view-as-scene" feature generation and the 3D-aware Q-Former is a novel contribution. The careful integration of multi-view information with pre-trained LLMs, avoiding fine-tuning of the LLM backbone, is a notable design choice. Furthermore, the modularity of the system (being able to use 2D, 3D, or both) offers additional flexibility.

*   **Significance:**  Improving 3D scene understanding is crucial for a wide range of applications, including robotics, augmented reality, and autonomous navigation. LLMs provide a powerful tool, but relying solely on 3D point clouds can be limiting. Therefore, Argus has the potential to improve performance in tasks that require a detailed understanding of complex indoor environments. The presented results, especially the performance gain over existing 3D-LMMs, show its significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of relying solely on 3D point clouds for scene understanding and provides a convincing rationale for incorporating multi-view images.
    *   **Well-designed framework:** The architecture of Argus, with its fusion module and 3D-aware Q-Former, appears to be carefully designed and justified.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The authors conduct extensive experiments on multiple tasks, including ablation studies to analyze the contribution of individual components.
    *   **State-of-the-art results:** The paper demonstrates superior performance compared to existing 3D-LMMs.
    *   **Modular Design:** The capability to handle either 2D multi-view, 3D point clouds or both as input gives additional flexibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited dataset:** The experiments primarily rely on the ScanNet dataset, which is known for its specific characteristics and biases related to indoor environments. Generalization to outdoor or significantly different indoor settings may be a concern.
    *   **Dependence on point cloud quality:** While Argus aims to compensate for information loss in point clouds, its performance might still be affected by the initial quality of the 3D reconstruction.
    *   **Spatial relations:** the paper admits to some difficulty in the model regarding spacial relations and directional cues.
    *   **Lack of in-depth analysis:** The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the specific types of scenes and objects where Argus performs particularly well and where it struggles. Further insights into the reasons for the performance gains over other methods would be valuable.

*   **Potential influence:** Argus can influence future research by demonstrating the benefits of incorporating multi-view images into 3D-LMMs. The proposed architecture and the "view-as-scene" feature representation might inspire new approaches to multi-modal fusion for 3D scene understanding. The modular nature also encourages others to explore different combinations of data modalities.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the identified strengths and weaknesses, the paper represents a solid contribution to the field of 3D scene understanding. The proposed framework is novel and shows promising results, which highlights the potential of multi-view images to complement 3D point clouds. The architecture's modular design, the use of pre-trained LLMs, and the comprehensive experiments strengthen the paper. However, the limitations related to dataset bias, dependence on point cloud quality, and the potential need for further in-depth analysis limit its overall impact. Therefore, while a solid contribution, it is not a groundbreaking advance.

### DMQ: Dissecting Outliers of Diffusion Models for Post-Training Quantization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12933v1)
- **Authors**: Dongyeun Lee, Jiwan Hur, Hyounguk Shon, Jae Young Lee, Junmo Kim
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have achieved remarkable success in image generation but come with significant computational costs, posing challenges for deployment in resource-constrained environments. Recent post-training quantization (PTQ) methods have attempted to mitigate this issue by focusing on the iterative nature of diffusion models. However, these approaches often overlook outliers, leading to degraded performance at low bit-widths. In this paper, we propose a DMQ which combines Learned Equivalent Scaling (LES) and channel-wise Power-of-Two Scaling (PTS) to effectively address these challenges. Learned Equivalent Scaling optimizes channel-wise scaling factors to redistribute quantization difficulty between weights and activations, reducing overall quantization error. Recognizing that early denoising steps, despite having small quantization errors, crucially impact the final output due to error accumulation, we incorporate an adaptive timestep weighting scheme to prioritize these critical steps during learning. Furthermore, identifying that layers such as skip connections exhibit high inter-channel variance, we introduce channel-wise Power-of-Two Scaling for activations. To ensure robust selection of PTS factors even with small calibration set, we introduce a voting algorithm that enhances reliability. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms existing works, especially at low bit-widths such as W4A6 (4-bit weight, 6-bit activation) and W4A8, maintaining high image generation quality and model stability. The code is available at https://github.com/LeeDongYeun/dmq.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DMQ: Dissecting Outliers of Diffusion Models for Post-Training Quantization":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of quantizing diffusion models for resource-constrained environments. It identifies that outliers in activations degrade performance in low-bit quantization and proposes DMQ, a post-training quantization (PTQ) framework unifying Learned Equivalent Scaling (LES) and channel-wise Power-of-Two Scaling (PTS). LES learns channel-wise scaling factors to balance quantization difficulty between weights and activations, while PTS addresses extreme outliers by scaling activations with power-of-two factors.  An adaptive timestep weighting scheme prioritizes learning in critical timesteps. A robust voting algorithm is introduced for selecting PTS factors, even with small calibration sets. The paper demonstrates significant performance improvements over existing methods, particularly at low bit-widths like W4A6.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines several existing techniques (Equivalent Scaling, Power-of-Two Scaling, timestep weighting) but their application to the specific problem of diffusion model quantization, along with the careful analysis of outliers in activations *and* weights across time steps and layers, introduces a new and valuable perspective. The adaptive timestep weighting based on observed quantization error behavior and the robust voting algorithm for PTS factor selection adds to the novelty. However, all components of their method exist in some form or another, either explicitly or indirectly.

*   **Significance:** Quantizing diffusion models is a crucial step towards deploying these models in real-world applications where computational resources are limited. The demonstrated improvement in low-bit quantization (especially achieving usable results at W4A6) is significant. The paper provides a solid analysis of outlier behavior in diffusion models, which can inform future research in this area. The clear implementation details and the public code repository enhance reproducibility and allow other researchers to build upon their work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Thorough analysis of the problem, particularly the impact of outliers and the importance of early timesteps.
    *   Effective combination of LES and PTS for handling different types of outliers.
    *   Adaptive timestep weighting scheme to prioritize critical timesteps during learning.
    *   Robust voting algorithm for PTS factor selection.
    *   Comprehensive experimental results demonstrating improvements over existing methods, especially at low bit-widths.
    *   Well-written and clear with good implementation details.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The individual components of DMQ (LES, PTS, timestep weighting) are not entirely novel in isolation. The major strength lies in how they are integrated and adapted to this specific application, which is slightly diminished by this lack of truly original elements.
    *   While the voting algorithm is presented as robust, the exact sensitivity of the performance to the K parameter (agreement threshold) could be explored more thoroughly.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a strong baseline for future research on quantizing diffusion models. The analysis of outliers and the proposed techniques can inspire other researchers to explore more efficient and effective quantization methods. The DMQ framework could potentially become a standard approach for quantizing diffusion models, particularly for resource-constrained applications.

* **Overall Score:** Considering the points made above, a score of 7 is awarded. Although existing techniques are smartly combined and applied to this setting, the individual components are incremental rather than revolutionary, which prevents the work from getting a significantly higher score.

### Analysis of Image-and-Text Uncertainty Propagation in Multimodal Large Language Models with Cardiac MR-Based Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12945v1)
- **Authors**: Yucheng Tang, Yunguan Fu, Weixi Yi, Yipei Wang, Daniel C. Alexander, Rhodri Davies, Yipeng Hu
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can process and integrate information from multimodality sources, such as text and images. However, interrelationship among input modalities, uncertainties due to individual uni-modal data and potential clinical applications following such an uncertainty decomposition are yet fully understood in the context of large-scale MLLMs. In this work, we propose a multimodal uncertainty propagation model (MUPM) based on uncertainty propagation, to characterise the relationship among the uncertainties arising from image-only, text-only, and joint image-text variations in MLLM inputs. Using real clinical data consisting of cardiac MR scans and digital health records, we describe that MUPMs can be optimised robustly with a few samples. We then show that the fitted MUPMs are generalisable across different input data distributions and, perhaps surprisingly, across different downstream tasks. Such a transferability may be explained by the shared pretraining, comparatively light MLLM fine-tuning, along with the low-dimensional nature of the MUPMs. More importantly, this learned transferability, quantifying the relationship between these uncertainties, led to direct clinical applications in which uncertainties may be estimated and thus analysed robustly for varying data or even a novel set of cardiac disease prediction tasks. In addition, we show experimentally the efficiency in multimodal data required for estimating the overall uncertainty and its ability to identify redundant factors, both of which are considered practical yet clinically useful applications with the proposed MUPMs. Codes are available at https://github.com/yucheng722/MUPM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Multimodal Uncertainty Propagation Model (MUPM) for analyzing uncertainty in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), specifically in the context of cardiac MR image analysis combined with patient digital health records.  The model aims to characterize the relationship between uncertainties arising from image-only, text-only, and combined image-text inputs.  Instead of directly computing partial derivatives, which are numerically unstable in large models, the paper proposes fitting a linear model to approximate the uncertainty propagation. The paper then presents four applications of MUPM for cardiac disease prediction: assessing robustness across different data distributions, evaluating performance on different downstream tasks, improving efficiency in estimating overall uncertainty, and identifying redundant input factors.  The experiments are conducted using a large dataset from the UK Biobank.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of this work lies in its application of uncertainty propagation to the *multimodal* domain within MLLMs, and its focus on *clinical applications* using cardiac MRI data. While uncertainty propagation itself isn't a new concept, adapting it to MLLMs, especially in the context of medical imaging and health records, adds a layer of innovation. Using regression coefficients as an approximation of the partial derivatives is also novel, as opposed to directly computing them. Furthermore, the idea of using MUPM for identifying redundant input factors provides potential value.

*   **Significance:** The work addresses a crucial gap in understanding how uncertainties in different modalities interact within MLLMs, especially in high-stakes medical applications. By providing a framework for quantifying and analyzing these uncertainties, the paper contributes to making MLLM predictions more reliable and trustworthy. The identified applications, such as adapting MLLMs to changing data quality or pinpointing redundant data elements, suggest practical benefits for clinical use.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper tackles an important problem in the context of MLLMs: uncertainty quantification in multimodal inputs.
    *   The MUPM approach offers a more stable and computationally feasible alternative to direct derivative computation.
    *   The experiments are conducted on a large clinical dataset from the UK Biobank, making the results relevant to real-world scenarios.
    *   The paper explores several potentially useful clinical applications for the MUPM framework.
    *   The provided code adds to the reproducibility and potential for further development of the work.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While fitting the MUPM is stable, it is essentially a linear approximation to a complex non-linear function of uncertainty from the MLLM. Although the method works, the justification for *why* it works could be further explored.
    *   The interpretation of the regression coefficients, especially in terms of identifying truly "redundant" factors, needs to be viewed with caution. Low coefficients might indicate complex non-linear interactions, rather than true redundancy.
    *   The closed-ended VQA task for cardiac disease prediction limits the expressiveness of the MLLM's capabilities. It could be more insightful to explore more open-ended tasks.
    *   The reliance on synonymous replacements for text augmentation might not fully capture the range of variations in real-world health records.
    * The paper is somewhat dense and could benefit from more clarity in explaining the methodology.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research on uncertainty-aware MLLMs in healthcare, particularly in medical image analysis. The MUPM framework could be adopted and extended by other researchers to analyze uncertainty in different MLLM architectures and medical domains. The identified applications could also inspire new methods for improving the reliability and interpretability of MLLM predictions in clinical settings.

* **Overall:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of multimodal machine learning by addressing uncertainty propagation in the specific context of cardiac imaging combined with health records. The proposed method is computationally feasible and appears relatively stable, but its reliance on linear approximation as an approximation to a much more complex process and over-interpretation of redundant parameters warrants some caution. Furthermore, the application to more complex tasks beyond closed-ended VQA is required for a more significant impact.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Probabilistic Soundness Guarantees in LLM Reasoning Chains
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12948v1)
- **Authors**: Weiqiu You, Anton Xue, Shreya Havaldar, Delip Rao, Helen Jin, Chris Callison-Burch, Eric Wong
- **Abstract**: In reasoning chains generated by large language models (LLMs), initial errors often propagate and undermine the reliability of the final conclusion. Current LLM-based error detection methods often fail to detect propagated errors because they do not properly account for how earlier errors might corrupt judgments of downstream reasoning. To better detect such propagated errors, we introduce Autoregressive Reasoning Entailment Stability (ARES), a novel probabilistic framework that prevents error propagation by judging each claim based only on previously-assessed sound premises. This inductive method yields a nuanced score for each step and provides certified statistical guarantees of its soundness, rather than a brittle binary label. ARES achieves state-of-the-art performance across four benchmarks (72.1% Macro-F1, +8.2 points) and demonstrates superior robustness on very long synthetic reasoning chains, where it excels at detecting propagated errors (90.3% F1, +27.6 points).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Autoregressive Reasoning Entailment Stability (ARES), a novel probabilistic framework for evaluating the soundness of reasoning chains generated by large language models (LLMs).  ARES addresses the problem of error propagation in these chains by inductively judging each claim based solely on previously validated, "sound" premises. This approach provides a nuanced soundness score for each step, offering statistical guarantees rather than brittle binary labels. The authors demonstrate that ARES achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple benchmarks, especially excelling in detecting propagated errors in long reasoning chains.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the *autoregressive* approach to soundness evaluation.  Instead of evaluating the entire chain holistically (as many LLM-Judge style methods do) or considering only base claims (which can ignore valid derivations from other derived claims), ARES progressively builds a set of trusted premises and only uses those for subsequent evaluations.  This mirrors, to some extent, how a human reviewer might approach a proof, and it's a significant departure from existing LLM-based error detection methods that treat each step independently or attempt a global analysis. The probabilistic element, allowing for nuanced evaluation and handling uncertainty is also a novel and valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a critical problem:  the untrustworthiness of LLM-generated reasoning. Error propagation undermines the entire chain, even if later steps are logically sound *given* the erroneous earlier steps. By effectively detecting these propagated errors, ARES contributes directly to making LLM reasoning more reliable and trustworthy. The authors successfully demonstrate significant improvements over existing baselines, showing the practical value of their approach. The rigorous evaluation across multiple benchmarks and the creation of targeted datasets (ClaimTrees, CaptainCookRecipes) to highlight specific weaknesses of existing methods strengthens the paper's argument. The theoretical guarantees are also a strong point.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel autoregressive approach:** The core idea is a significant advance in LLM error detection.
    *   **Strong empirical results:** ARES consistently outperforms baselines across a range of tasks and conditions, including very long reasoning chains.
    *   **Creation of targeted datasets:** These highlight the weaknesses of existing methods and demonstrate the strengths of ARES.
    *   **Theoretical guarantees:** Providing certified statistical guarantees for soundness is important for high-stakes applications.
    *   **Addresses a critical problem:** Enhancing the reliability and trustworthiness of LLM reasoning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on the entailment model:** The effectiveness of ARES hinges on the quality and calibration of the underlying entailment model. While the authors acknowledge this and propose model-agnosticism, the results still rely on specific LLMs (GPT-4o-mini, Qwen3-4B, etc). Poor entailment models would compromise ARES's performance. Further analysis of how the entailment model selection affect the results is suggested.
    *   **Computational cost:** While the paper proposes efficient sampling, ARES is inherently more computationally intensive than simpler methods.  The trade-off between accuracy and computational cost needs careful consideration in practical deployments.
    *   **Limited sub-claim granularity:** As mentioned in the limitations section, the paper can't detect errors at a sub-claim level.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of more robust and reliable LLM reasoning systems.  The autoregressive approach could be adopted and extended by other researchers. The framework of generating counterfactual reasoning chains and analyzing their effect on soundness score is valuable as well. The targeted datasets can become valuable resources for evaluating future error detection methods.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

ARES presents a significant and novel approach to a critical problem in LLM research: the reliability of reasoning chains. The strong empirical results and theoretical guarantees demonstrate the effectiveness of the method. The creation of targeted datasets further strengthens the evaluation. While the dependency on the underlying entailment model and the increased computational cost are important considerations, they don't overshadow the core contributions of the paper. A score of 8 reflects the paper's clear novelty, significance, and potential influence, tempered by the limitations noted above. The impact could be even higher as better entailment models are developed, and more sophisticated sampling techniques are devised to further reduce the computational overhead.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### UniSLU: Unified Spoken Language Understanding from Heterogeneous Cross-Task Datasets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12951v1)
- **Authors**: Zhichao Sheng, Shilin Zhou, Chen Gong, Zhenghua Li
- **Abstract**: Spoken Language Understanding (SLU) plays a crucial role in speech-centric multimedia applications, enabling machines to comprehend spoken language in scenarios such as meetings, interviews, and customer service interactions. SLU encompasses multiple tasks, including Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR), spoken Named Entity Recognition (NER), and spoken Sentiment Analysis (SA). However, existing methods often rely on separate model architectures for individual tasks such as spoken NER and SA, which increases system complexity, limits cross-task interaction, and fails to fully exploit heterogeneous datasets available across tasks. To address these limitations, we propose UniSLU, a unified framework that jointly models multiple SLU tasks within a single architecture. Specifically, we propose a unified representation for diverse SLU tasks, enabling full utilization of heterogeneous datasets across multiple tasks. Built upon this representation, we propose a unified generative method that jointly models ASR, spoken NER, and SA tasks, enhancing task interactions and enabling seamless integration with large language models to harness their powerful generative capabilities. Extensive experiments on public SLU datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach, achieving superior SLU performance compared to several benchmark methods, making it well-suited for real-world speech-based multimedia scenarios. We will release all code and models at github to facilitate future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniSLU, a unified framework for spoken language understanding (SLU) designed to address the limitations of existing methods that typically handle different SLU tasks (ASR, NER, SA) with separate models. UniSLU proposes a unified representation for diverse SLU tasks, enabling the model to leverage heterogeneous datasets across tasks. It employs a generative approach where the model jointly performs ASR, spoken NER, and SA within a single architecture. The framework uses a shared encoder (leveraging Whisper) and a decoder to generate a unified sequence containing the ASR transcript and the outputs of the NER and SA tasks. Experiments on SLUE-VoxPopuli and SLUE-VoxCeleb datasets demonstrate that UniSLU outperforms several baselines. The authors also explore integrating large language models (LLMs) into the framework to further improve performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unified Framework:** The paper's main strength is the unified generative framework for SLU. By jointly modeling ASR, NER, and SA, the model can potentially capture interactions between these tasks, leading to better overall performance and reducing the complexity of separate models.
    *   **Heterogeneous Data Usage:** The paper explicitly aims to leverage heterogeneous datasets across different SLU tasks, which is a practical and important consideration for real-world applications where fully annotated datasets for all tasks are rare. The proposed unified representation facilitates this data usage.
    *   **Integration with LLMs:** The approach allows for easy integration with LLMs, benefiting from their powerful generative capabilities and knowledge, potentially improving the semantic understanding of SLU tasks.
    *   **Experimental Results:** The experimental results on the SLUE datasets show promising improvements over several baselines, particularly in leveraging a larger Whisper model, supporting the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study is well-designed and provides valuable insights into the impact of different components, such as the dynamic loss mechanism and encoder fine-tuning.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the idea of a unified framework is appealing, the core components (Whisper encoder, generative modeling, using prefixes) are not entirely novel. The novelty comes from the specific combination and adaptation of these techniques for the SLU tasks considered.
    *   **Limited Novelty of LLM integration:** Combining generative models with LLMs is an established trend. The integration with a separate adapter, while practical, does not represent a significant architectural innovation. It lacks exploration of architectural changes to facilitate task interaction, such as different attention mechanisms.
    *   **Dependence on Whisper:** While leveraging Whisper is a practical choice, it also tightly couples the framework to a specific ASR model. The generalizability of the approach to other ASR backbones may be limited. The improvement may be more strongly linked to better ASR through Whisper, than improvements to NER or SA. The ASR score suffers when combining with LLMs due to limitations of the Whisper backbones, which is a crucial area for improvement.
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** The paper could benefit from a more detailed error analysis. Understanding the types of errors made by UniSLU compared to the baselines would provide more insights into the strengths and weaknesses of the approach.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** The evaluation mainly relies on standard metrics (WER, F1 score) and the SLUE composite metric. More analysis of robustness and generalization ability in different scenarios (e.g., noisy environments, different accents) would enhance the evaluation.
    *   **Scalability of LLMs:** The experiments with LLMs show performance improvements, but they are also computationally expensive. The paper does not fully address the scalability and practical implications of using larger LLMs in real-world SLU applications.

*   **Significance and Impact:**

    *   The paper contributes to the ongoing research on unified SLU systems. By demonstrating the benefits of a unified generative framework, it encourages further exploration of joint modeling approaches. The usage of accessible LLMs is significant as well.
    *   The focus on leveraging heterogeneous data is valuable for practical applications of SLU.
    *   The paper's results suggest that combining unified modeling with powerful LLMs can lead to significant improvements in SLU performance, potentially impacting various applications such as virtual assistants, meeting transcription, and customer service.

*   **Overall Assessment:**

    The paper proposes a practical and effective unified framework for SLU. While the novelty is somewhat incremental, the results are promising, and the framework addresses important challenges in the field. The integration with LLMs and the focus on heterogeneous data usage are particularly relevant. However, the paper could benefit from more architectural innovations, a deeper error analysis, and a more thorough exploration of the scalability and limitations of the approach.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

A score of 6.5 reflects the combination of the paper's strengths and weaknesses. The unified framework, heterogeneous data usage, and LLM integration are valuable contributions, but the incremental novelty, dependence on Whisper, and limited error analysis temper the overall impact. The paper provides solid empirical evidence, but further investigation and comparison against other existing techniques are needed to solidify its position as a significant advance in the field. The paper does demonstrate promising improvements over baselines, but further refinement is needed to reach a higher score.

- **Classification**: eess.AS
- **Score**: 6/10

### LoViC: Efficient Long Video Generation with Context Compression
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12952v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaxiu Jiang, Wenbo Li, Jingjing Ren, Yuping Qiu, Yong Guo, Xiaogang Xu, Han Wu, Wangmeng Zuo
- **Abstract**: Despite recent advances in diffusion transformers (DiTs) for text-to-video generation, scaling to long-duration content remains challenging due to the quadratic complexity of self-attention. While prior efforts -- such as sparse attention and temporally autoregressive models -- offer partial relief, they often compromise temporal coherence or scalability. We introduce LoViC, a DiT-based framework trained on million-scale open-domain videos, designed to produce long, coherent videos through a segment-wise generation process. At the core of our approach is FlexFormer, an expressive autoencoder that jointly compresses video and text into unified latent representations. It supports variable-length inputs with linearly adjustable compression rates, enabled by a single query token design based on the Q-Former architecture. Additionally, by encoding temporal context through position-aware mechanisms, our model seamlessly supports prediction, retradiction, interpolation, and multi-shot generation within a unified paradigm. Extensive experiments across diverse tasks validate the effectiveness and versatility of our approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LoViC: Efficient Long Video Generation with Context Compression":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LoViC, a DiT-based framework for generating long, coherent videos. LoViC addresses the computational limitations of applying diffusion transformers (DiTs) directly to long video sequences. It achieves this through a segment-wise generation process where each segment is conditioned on the full history of preceding segments. At the core of LoViC is FlexFormer, a Q-Former-based autoencoder that compresses video and text context into unified latent representations with adaptive compression rates.  The framework supports video prediction, retrodiction, interpolation, and multi-shot generation. The authors train the model on a million-scale open-domain video corpus and demonstrate its effectiveness across diverse tasks. Key innovations include the FlexFormer architecture with a single learnable query token and an Interpolated ROPE (I-ROPE) positional encoding scheme.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel components.  FlexFormer with its adaptive compression and I-ROPE is a significant contribution to efficiently encoding long-range dependencies. The unified framework supporting multiple video generation tasks (prediction, retrodiction, interpolation, multi-shot) is also novel. The application of a single-query token within a Q-Former architecture to achieve variable compression is a creative approach.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in video generation: scaling diffusion models to long sequences.  The quadratic complexity of self-attention has been a major bottleneck. LoViC's approach to context compression offers a promising path toward generating longer and more coherent videos, which has numerous real-world applications (e.g., gaming, simulations, user-generated content). The million-scale training corpus and the experiments across diverse tasks strengthen the significance of the work. The improvement over baseline models, particularly in terms of temporal consistency and subject identity preservation, demonstrates the practical value of the approach.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Effective Context Compression:** The core strength is FlexFormer's ability to compress long-range dependencies while maintaining temporal coherence and semantic consistency.
    * **Unified Framework:** The ability to perform prediction, retrodiction, interpolation, and multi-shot generation within a single framework adds to the versatility and practicality of the method.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** The qualitative and quantitative results on Panda-70M demonstrate significant improvements over existing methods.
    * **Well-Written and Clear:** The paper is well-organized and presents the concepts clearly.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Cost:** While LoViC addresses some computational challenges, the paper would benefit from a more thorough analysis of the computational cost compared to other long video generation methods. Quantifying the actual savings and scalability would further strengthen the claim of efficiency. The paper only looks at single time step inference in figure 2. Other approaches to improve computational cost by reducing the number of tokens are not compared.
    * **Reliance on VAE:** The method still relies on a VAE for latent representation, which is a standard approach but could be a limiting factor in terms of video quality. Alternatives should be considered.
    * **Limited Multi-Shot Data:** The limited availability of multi-shot video data is acknowledged, and the resulting lower Aesthetic Quality and Video-text Alignment scores compared to the base model are a point of concern. Future work should focus on improving results in this area.
    * **Incremental Improvement:** Some of the technical choices, while effective, are incremental improvements over existing techniques (e.g., M-ROPE). It would be good to see what innovations would push the field to new directions.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field of video generation. The FlexFormer architecture and the I-ROPE positional encoding scheme could be adopted in other video generation frameworks. The unified approach to different video generation tasks can inspire further research into more versatile and general-purpose models. The paper's focus on long-range dependencies and temporal coherence is essential, and LoViC offers a practical solution that can be further refined and extended.

* **Justification for Score:**
Considering the novelty of the FlexFormer architecture and its adaptive compression, the demonstrated improvements in temporal coherence and subject identity, and the versatility of the unified framework, the paper represents a notable contribution to the field. However, the limited multi-shot performance and a lack of detail regarding the computational complexity limit the overall impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### FantasyPortrait: Enhancing Multi-Character Portrait Animation with Expression-Augmented Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12956v1)
- **Authors**: Qiang Wang, Mengchao Wang, Fan Jiang, Yaqi Fan, Yonggang Qi, Mu Xu
- **Abstract**: Producing expressive facial animations from static images is a challenging task. Prior methods relying on explicit geometric priors (e.g., facial landmarks or 3DMM) often suffer from artifacts in cross reenactment and struggle to capture subtle emotions. Furthermore, existing approaches lack support for multi-character animation, as driving features from different individuals frequently interfere with one another, complicating the task. To address these challenges, we propose FantasyPortrait, a diffusion transformer based framework capable of generating high-fidelity and emotion-rich animations for both single- and multi-character scenarios. Our method introduces an expression-augmented learning strategy that utilizes implicit representations to capture identity-agnostic facial dynamics, enhancing the model's ability to render fine-grained emotions. For multi-character control, we design a masked cross-attention mechanism that ensures independent yet coordinated expression generation, effectively preventing feature interference. To advance research in this area, we propose the Multi-Expr dataset and ExprBench, which are specifically designed datasets and benchmarks for training and evaluating multi-character portrait animations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FantasyPortrait significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both quantitative metrics and qualitative evaluations, excelling particularly in challenging cross reenactment and multi-character contexts. Our project page is https://fantasy-amap.github.io/fantasy-portrait/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "FantasyPortrait: Enhancing Multi-Character Portrait Animation with Expression-Augmented Diffusion Transformers" introduces a novel framework for generating expressive facial animations from static images, particularly focusing on multi-character scenarios. The core contributions include: (1) an expression-augmented learning strategy using implicit representations to capture identity-agnostic facial dynamics and enhance emotion rendering, (2) a masked cross-attention mechanism to prevent feature interference and enable coordinated multi-character expression control, and (3) the creation of the Multi-Expr dataset and ExprBench benchmark for training and evaluating multi-character portrait animation. The framework leverages a Diffusion Transformer (DiT) architecture.  Experiments demonstrate superior performance compared to existing methods, especially in cross-identity reenactment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and challenging problem in portrait animation, namely generating high-fidelity and emotionally expressive animations, particularly in multi-character settings. The key strengths lie in its novel architectural components and the introduction of new datasets and benchmarks.

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The expression-augmented learning strategy is a worthwhile improvement. It moves beyond explicit geometric priors, potentially leading to more robust handling of identity differences and more nuanced emotion capture.  The use of implicit representations is not entirely novel in the broader field of generative models, but its specific application within a DiT architecture for portrait animation appears to be a significant advance.
    *   The masked cross-attention mechanism represents a clear improvement for multi-character animation.  It provides a principled way to prevent feature leakage and ensure independent yet coordinated expression control.
    *   The Multi-Expr dataset and ExprBench are valuable contributions to the community, addressing a current lack of resources for multi-character animation research.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The performance gains demonstrated in cross-reenactment scenarios indicate a practical advancement in handling real-world variations in facial structure and identity.
    *   The ability to generate coordinated animations across multiple characters opens up new possibilities for applications like virtual communication, gaming, and content creation.
    *   The proposed benchmark and dataset will likely stimulate further research in multi-character portrait animation, encouraging the development of more sophisticated and robust methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies on a DiT backbone (Wan et al. 2025).  While this isn't necessarily a weakness, the incremental contribution of the proposed method relative to this existing DiT architecture should be more clearly delineated. The description and evaluation need to strongly convince the reader what specific elements contribute to improvements, and to what degree.
    *   Ethical considerations related to the potential misuse of high-fidelity portrait animation for identity theft or deepfakes are acknowledged but could be discussed in more depth.
    *   The computational complexity of the proposed framework is not explicitly addressed. Given the use of DiTs and attention mechanisms, the method likely has high computational requirements, which could limit its widespread adoption.
    *   It would be good to include the link to the project page in the paper abstract.

*   **Impact:**
    *   The paper has the potential to influence future research in portrait animation, particularly in multi-character scenarios and cross-identity reenactment.
    *   The release of the Multi-Expr dataset and ExprBench could establish a new standard for evaluating multi-character animation methods.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a worthwhile contribution to the field of portrait animation, particularly through its novel architectural components (expression-augmented learning and masked cross-attention) and the creation of valuable datasets and benchmarks.  While it builds upon existing DiT architectures, the proposed method demonstrates significant performance gains, particularly in challenging cross-reenactment scenarios. The paper's significance is further strengthened by its focus on multi-character animation, an area that has been relatively under-explored.

However, the paper would benefit from a more detailed discussion of the computational complexity, a more nuanced breakdown of the architectural contributions, and a stronger commitment to addressing the ethical considerations related to high-fidelity animation.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### RGB Pre-Training Enhanced Unobservable Feature Latent Diffusion Model for Spectral Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12967v1)
- **Authors**: Keli Deng, Jie Nie, Yuntao Qian
- **Abstract**: Spectral reconstruction (SR) is a crucial problem in image processing that requires reconstructing hyperspectral images (HSIs) from the corresponding RGB images. A key difficulty in SR is estimating the unobservable feature, which encapsulates significant spectral information not captured by RGB imaging sensors. The solution lies in effectively constructing the spectral-spatial joint distribution conditioned on the RGB image to complement the unobservable feature. Since HSIs share a similar spatial structure with the corresponding RGB images, it is rational to capitalize on the rich spatial knowledge in RGB pre-trained models for spectral-spatial joint distribution learning. To this end, we extend the RGB pre-trained latent diffusion model (RGB-LDM) to an unobservable feature LDM (ULDM) for SR. As the RGB-LDM and its corresponding spatial autoencoder (SpaAE) already excel in spatial knowledge, the ULDM can focus on modeling spectral structure. Moreover, separating the unobservable feature from the HSI reduces the redundant spectral information and empowers the ULDM to learn the joint distribution in a compact latent space. Specifically, we propose a two-stage pipeline consisting of spectral structure representation learning and spectral-spatial joint distribution learning to transform the RGB-LDM into the ULDM. In the first stage, a spectral unobservable feature autoencoder (SpeUAE) is trained to extract and compress the unobservable feature into a 3D manifold aligned with RGB space. In the second stage, the spectral and spatial structures are sequentially encoded by the SpeUAE and the SpaAE, respectively. The ULDM is then acquired to model the distribution of the coded unobservable feature with guidance from the corresponding RGB images. Experimental results on SR and downstream relighting tasks demonstrate that our proposed method achieves state-of-the-art performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel spectral reconstruction (SR) method called "Unobservable Feature Latent Diffusion Model" (ULDM) that aims to reconstruct hyperspectral images (HSIs) from corresponding RGB images. The core idea is to leverage pre-trained spatial knowledge from RGB models, specifically the RGB-LDM, and to model the "unobservable feature" – the spectral information missing in RGB images – separately.  The approach uses a two-stage training process: (1) spectral structure representation learning via a spectral unobservable feature autoencoder (SpeUAE) and (2) spectral-spatial joint distribution learning using a fine-tuned LDM conditioned on both RGB and the extracted unobservable features.  The ULDM aims to capture the joint distribution of spectral and spatial information more effectively by focusing on the "unobservable" part, while the pre-trained RGB-LDM handles the known spatial aspects. The results demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on SR and downstream relighting tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific way it combines a pre-trained RGB-LDM with a dedicated modeling of the unobservable feature space in spectral reconstruction. The two-stage training approach, separating spectral structure representation from joint distribution learning, is a notable architectural contribution. While the individual components (LDMs, autoencoders) are not novel on their own, their synergistic combination in this specific SR context is a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its potential to address the limitations of existing SR methods. Specifically, it addresses the difficulty of learning the complex joint spectral-spatial distribution by exploiting the rich spatial knowledge present in pre-trained RGB models. Furthermore, by modeling the unobservable features separately, the method potentially allows for a more efficient and robust reconstruction, requiring less training data. The improved relighting performance is a significant validation of the reconstructed HSIs' fidelity.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results: The paper presents convincing experimental results across multiple datasets, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance compared to existing SR techniques and shows improvements on the downstream task of relighting.
    *   Well-motivated approach: The approach is well-motivated, clearly articulating the challenges of SR and explaining how the proposed ULDM addresses these challenges by exploiting the spatial knowledge in RGB pre-trained models and by isolating unobservable feature modeling.
    *   Ablation studies: The paper includes ablation studies that demonstrate the effectiveness of the various components of the proposed method.
    *   Downstream Application: Testing on relighting highlights a real-world application where the reconstructed images from the proposed method can improve over the existing ones.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Complexity:  The two-stage training process adds complexity compared to end-to-end SR methods. While the paper argues this is necessary for performance, it could increase the computational cost and training time.
    *   Dependence on RGB-LDM: The method relies on the availability of a well-trained RGB-LDM. While RGB-LDMs are becoming more common, this dependence could limit the method's applicability in some scenarios. Also the choice of the diffusion model and its implementation details are not justified.
    *   Limited analysis of the "unobservable feature": While the paper emphasizes the importance of modeling the unobservable feature, there is limited analysis of *what* this feature actually represents in the learned latent space. Understanding the characteristics of the learned unobservable feature could further improve the method and provide insights into the SR problem.
    *   The authors should provide the parameters/number of parameters of their models and compare with other existing SR methods. This is a key point to measure the performance/computational cost against existing SR methods.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in SR and related areas. The idea of leveraging pre-trained models and explicitly modeling the "unobservable" components in inverse problems is a valuable concept that could be applied to other domains. Furthermore, the two-stage training approach could be adopted and adapted for other scenarios where it is beneficial to separate different aspects of the learning process.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of spectral reconstruction, with a novel and well-motivated approach that achieves state-of-the-art performance. The strengths of the paper (empirical results, clear motivation, ablation studies) outweigh the weaknesses (complexity, reliance on pre-trained models). The influence of the paper will depend on the degree to which other researchers adopt the ULDM approach and the extent to which it generalizes to other inverse problems. This solid contribution, which builds on previously published work while offering a distinct and innovative method justifies a rating of 7.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Non-differentiable Reward Optimization for Diffusion-based Autonomous Motion Planning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12977v1)
- **Authors**: Giwon Lee, Daehee Park, Jaewoo Jeong, Kuk-Jin Yoon
- **Abstract**: Safe and effective motion planning is crucial for autonomous robots. Diffusion models excel at capturing complex agent interactions, a fundamental aspect of decision-making in dynamic environments. Recent studies have successfully applied diffusion models to motion planning, demonstrating their competence in handling complex scenarios and accurately predicting multi-modal future trajectories. Despite their effectiveness, diffusion models have limitations in training objectives, as they approximate data distributions rather than explicitly capturing the underlying decision-making dynamics. However, the crux of motion planning lies in non-differentiable downstream objectives, such as safety (collision avoidance) and effectiveness (goal-reaching), which conventional learning algorithms cannot directly optimize. In this paper, we propose a reinforcement learning-based training scheme for diffusion motion planning models, enabling them to effectively learn non-differentiable objectives that explicitly measure safety and effectiveness. Specifically, we introduce a reward-weighted dynamic thresholding algorithm to shape a dense reward signal, facilitating more effective training and outperforming models trained with differentiable objectives. State-of-the-art performance on pedestrian datasets (CrowdNav, ETH-UCY) compared to various baselines demonstrates the versatility of our approach for safe and effective motion planning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a key limitation in diffusion-based motion planning: the inability to directly optimize non-differentiable objectives like safety (collision avoidance) and effectiveness (goal reaching).  The authors propose a Reinforcement Learning (RL) based training scheme called DDPO (Denoising Diffusion Policy Optimization) that integrates non-differentiable reward signals into the diffusion model.  They introduce a "reward-weighted dynamic thresholding" algorithm to address the problem of sparse rewards, adjusting reward thresholds to extract meaningful signals from more samples during training.  Experiments on pedestrian datasets (CrowdNav, ETH-UCY) demonstrate that their approach outperforms models trained with differentiable objectives, improving both safety and effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using RL to train diffusion models for motion planning isn't entirely new, as DDPO already exists. However, the specific application to motion planning with a focus on non-differentiable rewards *and* the introduction of the dynamic thresholding method to handle sparse rewards contribute a significant and practical novel component to this field. The way they are able to leverage DDPO to explicitly train models for motion planning, using rewards like collision avoidance, is quite innovative.  The novelty is more in the combination of existing techniques to solve a specific problem in diffusion-based motion planning.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in tackling a practical bottleneck in applying diffusion models to real-world robotics: directly optimizing for critical safety and performance metrics. Traditional diffusion models, trained primarily for data generation, often fall short in ensuring safety or efficiency. The proposed method allows for more robust and reliable motion planning, potentially enabling the use of diffusion models in safety-critical applications.  The significant performance improvements reported on standard benchmarks underscore the practical relevance of this work.

* **Strengths:**
    * The paper clearly identifies a real-world problem in current diffusion-based motion planning.
    * The proposed reward-weighted dynamic thresholding is a reasonable and potentially effective approach to address the sparse reward issue.
    * Strong experimental results demonstrate significant improvements over baseline methods, including those using differentiable approximations.
    * Ablation studies help validate the contribution of the dynamic thresholding algorithm.
    * The paper is well-written and structured, making it easy to understand the approach and results.
    * Flexibility by optimzing for various metrics and even complex scenarios shown with qualitative results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * While DDPO is a base, the paper could better explicitly acknowledge the dependencies on existing RL and diffusion techniques and more extensively describe those components for better comprehensibility.
    * Although the paper mentions the flexibility of the approach to optimize for multiple rewards (e.g., comfort), results could be strengthened with quantitative ablation studies.
    * Hyperparameter tuning for the dynamic thresholding algorithm may require significant effort in practice. More insights or guidelines on setting these parameters would improve the practical applicability of the method.
    * The complexity of the RL training may introduce additional computational overhead compared to traditional diffusion model training. This aspect is not thoroughly discussed.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical approach for incorporating safety and performance criteria into diffusion-based motion planning. This could lead to the development of more robust and reliable autonomous systems. The approach is versatile and can be applied to various planning tasks and scenarios.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the dependencies on DDPO, the practical problem of directly optimizing non-differentiable metrics, the clever solution to sparse rewards via dynamic thresholding, and the strong experimental results justify a score of **7**. The paper offers a significant engineering contribution to the field and addresses a practical concern within the community. Although the core algorithm isn't entirely novel as the base is from DDPO, the application and adaptation to the domain of motion planning and safety are strong contributions and makes the approach more useful for other motion planning scenarios. It has demonstrated a good impact for future robotics tasks.

### A Distributed Generative AI Approach for Heterogeneous Multi-Domain Environments under Data Sharing constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12979v1)
- **Authors**: Youssef Tawfilis, Hossam Amer, Minar El-Aasser, Tallal Elshabrawy
- **Abstract**: Federated Learning has gained increasing attention for its ability to enable multiple nodes to collaboratively train machine learning models without sharing their raw data. At the same time, Generative AI -- particularly Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) -- have achieved remarkable success across a wide range of domains, such as healthcare, security, and Image Generation. However, training generative models typically requires large datasets and significant computational resources, which are often unavailable in real-world settings. Acquiring such resources can be costly and inefficient, especially when many underutilized devices -- such as IoT devices and edge devices -- with varying capabilities remain idle. Moreover, obtaining large datasets is challenging due to privacy concerns and copyright restrictions, as most devices are unwilling to share their data. To address these challenges, we propose a novel approach for decentralized GAN training that enables the utilization of distributed data and underutilized, low-capability devices while not sharing data in its raw form. Our approach is designed to tackle key challenges in decentralized environments, combining KLD-weighted Clustered Federated Learning to address the issues of data heterogeneity and multi-domain datasets, with Heterogeneous U-Shaped split learning to tackle the challenge of device heterogeneity under strict data sharing constraints -- ensuring that no labels or raw data, whether real or synthetic, are ever shared between nodes. Experimental results shows that our approach demonstrates consistent and significant improvements across key performance metrics, where it achieves 1.1x -- 2.2x higher image generation scores, an average 10% boost in classification metrics (up to 50% in multi-domain non-IID settings), in much lower latency compared to several benchmarks. Find our code at https://github.com/youssefga28/HuSCF-GAN.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel distributed generative AI approach called Heterogeneous U-shaped Split Clustered Federated GANs (HuSCF-GANs) for training generative models in heterogeneous multi-domain environments under strict data sharing constraints. It addresses challenges like data heterogeneity (non-IID data), device heterogeneity (varying computational power), multi-domain datasets, and privacy concerns (no raw data or labels shared). The approach combines KLD-weighted Clustered Federated Learning and Heterogeneous U-Shaped split learning.  Experiments on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, KMNIST, and NotMNIST datasets show improved image generation scores, classification metrics, and reduced latency compared to other decentralized GAN frameworks.  A genetic algorithm optimizes the model's split between clients and the server, and clustered federated learning is used to address data heterogeneity, while intermediate activations and gradients are communicated.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines several existing techniques (federated learning, split learning, cGANs, genetic algorithms, and KLD divergence weighting) in a novel way. The *U-shaped split learning* configuration and the integration of *KLD weighting within a clustered federated learning framework* are potentially significant contributions. The genetic algorithm-based optimization for client-specific cut points is also a valuable aspect. What strengthens this combination is the data sharing constraint focus.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its ability to train generative models in realistic, privacy-sensitive distributed environments. This is becoming increasingly important due to the growing amount of data generated at the edge and concerns about data security.  The results demonstrate tangible improvements in performance and latency, which could make decentralized GANs more practical for real-world applications, such as federated healthcare or distributed anomaly detection. The extensive experiments provide strong empirical evidence to support the claims, including single-domain IID and Non-IID, two-domain and four-domain settings with varied heterogeneity. That being said, these benchmarks have been criticized in the recent literature.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive approach addressing multiple challenges simultaneously.
    *   Addresses a relevant and timely problem.
    *   Demonstrates strong experimental results.
    *   Clear and well-structured writing.
    *   Code is publicly available, promoting reproducibility and future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The approach relies on a genetic algorithm for cut-point optimization, which can be computationally expensive, especially with a large number of clients. While the paper uses approximation, it might still be a bottleneck in certain scenarios.
    *   The paper lacks a deeper theoretical analysis.
    *   Evaluation uses established benchmarks (MNIST, FMNIST etc). Performance in real world scenarios, in particular with the presence of adversarial attacks is not examined.
    *   While the paper emphasizes privacy, a more rigorous analysis of the privacy guarantees provided by the approach, potentially using differential privacy or similar frameworks, would be beneficial. A threat model should have been included.
    *   The paper touches on real world applications in the conclusion, but does not specify them or dive into their possible nuances.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in decentralized generative AI, particularly in the areas of data privacy, model optimization, and heterogeneous learning. The approach could serve as a foundation for developing more practical and efficient distributed GAN frameworks.

*   **Rigorous Rationale**: The paper tackles a challenging problem in a robust manner, producing convincing evidence of improvements over existing techniques. The combination of techniques is genuinely innovative and will potentially spark interest from researchers facing similar challenges in decentralized and privacy-preserving machine learning. The paper provides a detailed analysis with multiple experimental setup and test case. Some of the claims in the abstract and conclusion can be considered aggressive. Although, some of the limitations were discussed previously, not adressing some known privacy concerns makes it difficult to use out-of-the-box in real life applications. For instance, the paper does not address Membership Inference Attacks.

### From Variability To Accuracy: Conditional Bernoulli Diffusion Models with Consensus-Driven Correction for Thin Structure Segmentation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12985v1)
- **Authors**: Jinseo An, Min Jin Lee, Kyu Won Shim, Helen Hong
- **Abstract**: Accurate segmentation of orbital bones in facial computed tomography (CT) images is essential for the creation of customized implants for reconstruction of defected orbital bones, particularly challenging due to the ambiguous boundaries and thin structures such as the orbital medial wall and orbital floor. In these ambiguous regions, existing segmentation approaches often output disconnected or under-segmented results. We propose a novel framework that corrects segmentation results by leveraging consensus from multiple diffusion model outputs. Our approach employs a conditional Bernoulli diffusion model trained on diverse annotation patterns per image to generate multiple plausible segmentations, followed by a consensus-driven correction that incorporates position proximity, consensus level, and gradient direction similarity to correct challenging regions. Experimental results demonstrate that our method outperforms existing methods, significantly improving recall in ambiguous regions while preserving the continuity of thin structures. Furthermore, our method automates the manual process of segmentation result correction and can be applied to image-guided surgical planning and surgery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework for segmenting orbital bones in facial CT images, specifically addressing the challenges of segmenting thin structures like the orbital medial wall and orbital floor, which often suffer from ambiguous boundaries and inter-observer variability in manual annotations. The framework combines a conditional Bernoulli diffusion model with a consensus-driven correction method. The diffusion model is trained on multiple annotations per image to learn inter-observer variability and generate plausible segmentations. The consensus-driven correction refines these segmentations by considering position proximity, consensus level, and gradient direction similarity, thus correcting challenging regions. Experimental results demonstrate that the method outperforms existing CNN-based and diffusion-based methods in terms of Dice score, recall, and precision, particularly in the orbital medial wall.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Combining Diffusion Models with Consensus:** The core idea of combining a diffusion model with consensus-driven correction is reasonably novel in the context of orbital bone segmentation.  While diffusion models have gained traction, the specific strategy for handling inter-observer variability and ambiguous boundaries using consensus-driven refinement is a valuable addition.
*   **Conditional Bernoulli Diffusion:** Using a Bernoulli diffusion model instead of a Gaussian one is more appropriate for mask segmentation tasks and is also more appropriate, as Bernoulli noise is discrete (0 or 1). This is a fairly straightforward adaptation, but relevant.
*   **Consensus-Driven Correction details:** The consensus-driven correction incorporating proximity, consensus level, and gradient direction is a key contribution. This helps in maintaining continuity of thin structures and correcting disconnected regions.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing a Practical Problem:**  Accurate orbital bone segmentation is essential for creating patient-specific implants and surgical planning, a clear clinical need. The paper directly tackles a relevant problem.
*   **Improved Accuracy:** The reported improvements in DSC, recall, and precision, particularly in thin bone regions, are significant. This suggests the proposed method is genuinely better at dealing with the difficulties that arise in these regions. The statistical significance is explicitly stated.
*   **Automation and Reduced Manual Correction:** The method automates segmentation result correction, potentially reducing the manual effort required in surgical planning.
*   **Limitations:** While the results are promising, the experimental setup is still somewhat limited by the size of the dataset (71 patients with 57 in the training set and 14 in the testing set). It would be beneficial to see performance on a larger, more diverse dataset.

**Strengths:**

*   Clearly articulated problem and motivation.
*   Well-structured methodology with detailed descriptions of each component.
*   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with comparisons to several existing methods.
*   Qualitative results that visually demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed correction method.
*   Statistical significance reported.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Relatively small dataset, which could limit the generalizability of the results.
*   The novelty, while present, is incremental.
*   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the computational cost associated with the diffusion model and consensus-driven correction.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field of medical image segmentation, particularly in applications involving thin or ambiguous structures. The combination of diffusion models and consensus-driven correction provides a promising approach for addressing inter-observer variability and improving segmentation accuracy. Other researchers and practitioners can build upon this work by applying similar techniques to other anatomical structures and exploring different consensus metrics.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable and well-executed approach to a clinically relevant problem. It introduces a novel combination of a diffusion model and consensus-driven correction, resulting in improved segmentation accuracy in challenging regions. While the dataset size is a limitation and the approach builds on existing techniques, the paper provides a solid contribution to the field of medical image segmentation and has the potential to influence future research and clinical applications. The improvement in recall is very important and signifies its improved performance in ambiguous regions.

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Teach Old SAEs New Domain Tricks with Boosting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12990v1)
- **Authors**: Nikita Koriagin, Yaroslav Aksenov, Daniil Laptev, Gleb Gerasimov, Nikita Balagansky, Daniil Gavrilov
- **Abstract**: Sparse Autoencoders have emerged as powerful tools for interpreting the internal representations of Large Language Models, yet they often fail to capture domain-specific features not prevalent in their training corpora. This paper introduces a residual learning approach that addresses this feature blindness without requiring complete retraining. We propose training a secondary SAE specifically to model the reconstruction error of a pretrained SAE on domain-specific texts, effectively capturing features missed by the primary model. By summing the outputs of both models during inference, we demonstrate significant improvements in both LLM cross-entropy and explained variance metrics across multiple specialized domains. Our experiments show that this method efficiently incorporates new domain knowledge into existing SAEs while maintaining their performance on general tasks. This approach enables researchers to selectively enhance SAE interpretability for specific domains of interest, opening new possibilities for targeted mechanistic interpretability of LLMs.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces "SAE Boost," a novel residual learning approach for enhancing Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) used in Large Language Model (LLM) interpretability. The core idea is to train a secondary SAE to model the reconstruction error of a pre-trained SAE on domain-specific data, allowing the capture of features missed by the primary model without complete retraining. The authors demonstrate improvements in LLM cross-entropy and explained variance across multiple domains (chemistry, Russian texts, and UN debates) and different LLM architectures (Qwen and Llama), while maintaining performance on general tasks. The method also enables the modular addition of multiple domain adaptations. They compare with other domain adaptation baselines, highlighting benefits in terms of domain-specific performance, sparsity, and avoidance of catastrophic forgetting. Finally, they showcase qualitative examples and analyze feature similarity between the boosted SAEs and the base SAE.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable and practical contribution to the field of mechanistic interpretability. The idea of using a residual SAE to augment existing models without retraining them is clever and addresses a critical limitation: feature blindness when dealing with domain-specific data. The empirical evaluation is comprehensive, covering multiple domains, model architectures, and metrics. The comparison with various domain adaptation baselines provides a good benchmark and highlights the strengths of the proposed approach, particularly in terms of maintaining general performance while enhancing domain-specific capabilities.

However, the paper could be improved in the following ways:

*   **Novelty:** While the idea of residual learning is not entirely new, its application in this specific context – augmenting SAEs for LLM interpretability – is a novel and practical contribution. The paper adequately positions the work within the existing literature on sparse coding and dictionary learning.
*   **Significance:** The approach addresses a real-world problem in mechanistic interpretability: the inability of general-purpose SAEs to capture domain-specific concepts. The results demonstrate that SAE Boost can effectively enhance interpretability in targeted domains without compromising overall performance. This could facilitate the development of more comprehensive tools for understanding LLMs and their limitations.
*   **Further Analysis:** While the qualitative examples are helpful, a more quantitative analysis of feature similarity or disentanglement would strengthen the paper. Additional ablation studies, such as varying the size of the residual SAE or exploring different sparsity constraints, would also be valuable.
*   **Theoretical justification:** A more detailed justification of why this method works, with perhaps a slight mathematical formalization of the reconstruction error landscape and how the residual model navigates it, could increase the appeal to a theoretical audience.

Overall, the paper provides a solid contribution to the field, offering a practical and effective solution to an important problem. The presented methodology is novel and well-supported by empirical findings, enhancing the efficiency and applicability of mechanistic interpretability techniques for LLMs.
